\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
intersects the special fiber of
\mathcal{N}_{n,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Kra}}
at a unique
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}
for some
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
. Moreover

\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }M^{\flat}\text{ is unimodular},\\
2&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

By the definition of
\mathrm{Hor}
, we can find a decomposition of
M^{\flat}

M^{\flat}=M\obot\{{\bf x}\}

such that
M
is self-dual. Let
\Lambda
be any vertex lattice containing
M^{\flat}
.
If
M^{\flat}
is unimodular, then
\Lambda
has to be of the form
M^{\flat}\obot L^{\prime}
where
L^{\prime}
is the unique unimodular lattice in
(M^{\flat}_{F})^{\bot}
. If
M^{\flat}
is of the form
M\obot L^{\prime}
such that
M
is of rank
n-2
and
(M_{F})^{\bot}
is non-split, then the proof of
[
Shi18
, Theorem 3.10]
implies that there is a unique vertex lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
in
(M_{F})^{\bot}
which is of unimodular (this fact is the same as the fact that the Bruhat-Tits building of
(M_{F})^{\bot}
has only one point). Then
\Lambda
must be of the form
M\obot\Lambda^{\prime}
. In both cases,
\Lambda
is unique and is of type
0
.

Assume
\chi(M)=\eta
. By Proposition
2.6
,
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M)\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\epsilon\eta}
. Moreover
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M)\cap\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
is an exceptional divisor in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\epsilon\eta}
. Hence by Lemma
3.1
, we have

\chi(\mathcal{N}_{n,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda}})=\chi(\mathcal{N}_{2,\epsilon\eta}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}}\otimes_{\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\epsilon\eta}}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}}).

Now the lemma follows from
[
HSY23
, Lemma 5.2]
when
\epsilon\eta=1
, and from
[
Shi22
, Proposition 3.11]
when
\epsilon\eta=-1
.
∎

4.3.
The horizontal part of special difference cycles

Definition
2.15
motivates us to make the following definition.

Definition 4.5
.

When
L^{\flat}
is a rank
n-1
integral lattice, define
\mathcal{D}(L^{\flat})_{h}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{n-1}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
by

(4.7)

\mathcal{D}(L^{\flat})_{h}=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{h}}]+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset\frac{1}{\pi}L^{\flat}\\
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(L^{\prime}/L^{\flat})=i\end{subarray}}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\prime})_{h}}].

Proposition 4.6
.

Assume
L^{\flat}
is a rank
n-1
integral lattice, then

\mathcal{D}(L^{\flat})_{h}=\begin{cases}\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}&\text{ if }L^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor},\\
0&\text{ if }L^{\flat}\notin\mathrm{Hor}.\end{cases}

Proof.

By Theorem
4.2
, it suffices to compute the multiplicity of an irreducible component in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
in
\mathcal{D}(L)_{h}
for all
M^{\flat}\in\mathrm{Hor}(L^{\flat})
(see (
4.3
)). For such a
M^{\flat}
, define

M^{\prime}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\pi}L^{\flat}\cap M^{\flat}\text{ and }m\coloneqq\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(M^{\prime}/L^{\flat}).

Then for a lattice
L^{\prime}
with
L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset\frac{1}{\pi}L^{\flat}
, we know that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
is in
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\prime})_{h}
if and only if
L^{\prime}\subset M^{\prime}
. Hence the multiplicity of an irreducible components in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})^{\circ}
in
\mathcal{D}(L)_{h}
is

(4.8)

1+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\flat}\subset L^{\prime}\subset M^{\prime}\\
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(L^{\prime}/L^{\flat})=i\end{subarray}}1.

Notice that
m=0
if and only if
M^{\prime}=M^{\flat}=L^{\flat}
, in this case the summation in (
4.8
) is over an empty set, hence (
4.8
) is equal to
1
.
If
m>0
, (
4.8
) is equal to
0
by (
2.13
).
∎

Part II
The analytic side

5.
Induction formula and primitive local density

In this section, we study various induction formulas of local density polynomials.
Let
M
be a Hermitian
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice of rank
m
with
\mathrm{v}(M)\coloneqq\mathrm{min}\{\mathrm{v}_{\pi}(h(v,v^{\prime}))\mid v,v^{\prime}\in M\}\geq-1
. and let
M^{[k]}=\mathcal{H}^{k}\obot M
for an integer
k\geq 0
. Let
L
be a Hermitian
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice of rank
n
.

There is a polynomial
\alpha(M,L,X)
of
X
—the local density polynomial—such that

(5.1)

\alpha(M,L,q^{-2k})=\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T\rangle)d{\bf x}\,dY,

where
T({\bf x})
is the moment matrix of
{\bf x}
,
d{\bf x}
is the Haar measure on
(M^{[k]})^{n}
with total volume
1
,
dY
is the Haar measures on
\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)
such that
\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
has total volume
1
, and
\psi
is an additive character of
F_{0}
with conductor
\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}
. Finally, we define
\langle X,Y\rangle=\mathrm{Tr}(XY)
on
\mathrm{Herm}_{n}
. We also use the notation
\alpha(M,L)=\alpha(M,L,1)
and

(5.2)

\alpha^{\prime}(M,L)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial X}\alpha(M,L,X)|_{X=1}.

There is another way to define
\alpha(M,L,X)
as follows. We use
\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}
to denote the scheme of Hermitian
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-module homomorphisms from
L
to
M
, which is a scheme of finite type over
\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}
. More specifically, for an
\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}
-algebra
R
, we define

\displaystyle L_{R}\coloneqq L\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}}R,\quad(x\otimes a,y\otimes b)_{R}\coloneqq\pi(x,y)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}}ab\in\mathcal{O}_{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}}R\text{ where }x,y\in L,a,b\in R.

Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}(R)=\{\phi\in\mathrm{Hom}

{}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}(L_{R},M_{R})\mid(\phi(x),\phi(y))_{R}\equiv(x,y)_{R}\text{ for all }x,y\in L_{R}\}.

To simplify the notation, we let

(5.3)

\displaystyle I(M,L,d)\coloneqq\mathrm{Herm}_{L,M}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}/(\pi_{0}^{d})).

Then a direct calculation as in
[
Shi22
, Lemma 6.1]
shows that

(5.4)

\alpha(M,L)=q^{-dn(2m-n)}|I(M,L,d)|

for sufficiently large integers
d>0
. Since
\alpha(M,L,X)
only depends on the Gram matrices of
M
and
L
, we may also denote it by
\alpha(S,T,X)
if
S
and
T
are the Gram matrices of
M
and
L
.

Now we define primitive local density polynomials. For
1\leq\ell\leq n
, let

(5.5)

\displaystyle(M^{[k]})^{n,(\ell)}=\{{\bf x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})\in(M^{[k]})^{n}\mid\dim\hbox{Span}\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{\ell}\}=\ell\hbox{ in }M^{[k]}/\pi M^{[k]}\}.

For

L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
, where
L_{1}=\mathrm{Span}\{l_{1},\cdots,l_{\ell}\}
and
L_{2}=\mathrm{Span}\{l_{\ell+1},\cdots,l_{n}\}
, we define the local
\ell
-primitive density to be

(5.6)

\beta(M^{[k]},L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(\ell)}=\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(\ell)}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T\rangle)d{\bf x}\,dY.

When
\ell\not=n
, the above definition depends on a choice of
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
. Hence we always fix such a decomposition
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
in this case. When
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
, and
L_{i}
is represented by
T_{i}
, we also denote
\beta(M,L_{1}\obot L_{2})^{(\ell)}
as
\beta(S,\mathrm{Diag}(T_{1},T_{2}))^{(\ell)}
. When
\ell=n
, we simply denote
\beta(M,L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(\ell)}
as
\beta(M,L)
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
where
\mathrm{rank}(L_{1})=n_{1}
. Then

\alpha(M,L,X)=\sum_{L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}}(q^{n-m}X)^{\ell(L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1})}\beta(M,L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2},X)^{(n_{1})},

where
\ell(L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1})=\mathrm{length}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1}
.

Proof.

This is the analogue of
[
Kit83
, Lemma 3]
. Let

G=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}(F)\cap\mathrm{M}_{n_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
and
U=\mathrm{GL}_{n_{1}}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. By choosing a basis
\{l_{1},\cdots,l_{n_{1}}\}
of
L_{1}
, we may identify
U\backslash G
with
\{L_{1}^{\prime}\mid L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}\}
by sending
g
to
L_{1}\cdot g^{-1}
. Then the identity we want to prove is equivalent to

\alpha(M,L,X)=\sum_{g\in U\backslash G}|\det g|^{2k+m-n}\beta(M,L_{1}\cdot g^{-1}\obot L_{2},X)^{(n_{1})},

where
|\pi|=q^{-1}.

By a partition of
M^{n}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(M,L,X)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in U\backslash G}\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}\cdot g_{1}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T\rangle)d{\bf x},

where
g_{1}=\mathrm{Diag}(g,I_{n-n_{1}})
, and the action of
g_{1}
is simply matrix multiplication on the
n
components of
M^{n,(n_{1})}
. Now

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}\cdot g_{1}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=|\det g_{1}|^{2k+m}\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x}g_{1})-T\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=|\det g_{1}|^{2k+m}\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}}\psi(\langle Y,(T({\bf x})-T[g_{1}^{-1}])[g_{1}]\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=|\det g_{1}|^{2k+m}\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}}\psi(\langle Y[g_{1}^{*}],T({\bf x})-T[g_{1}^{-1}]\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=|\det g_{1}|^{2k+m-n}\int_{\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)}dY\int_{(M^{[k]})^{n,(n_{1})}}\psi(\langle Y,T({\bf x})-T[g_{1}^{-1}]\rangle)d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=|\det g_{1}|^{2k+m-n}\beta(M^{[k]},L\cdot g_{1}^{-1})^{(n_{1})}.

Here
T[g]\coloneqq g^{*}Tg
. Now the lemma is clear.
∎

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
L
be as in Lemma
5.1
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha(M,L,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}(-1)^{i-1}q^{i(i-1)/2+i(n-m)}X^{i}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}L_{1}\\
\dim{L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1}}=i\end{subarray}}\alpha(M,L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2},X)+\beta(M,L,X)^{(n_{1})}.

Proof.

This is an analogue of
[
Kat99
, Proposition 2.1]
.
The proof follows from a combination of the argument (in a reverse order) in
2.16
and Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Motivated by Theorem
5.2
, we give the following definition.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
be as in Lemma
5.1
. We define

(5.7)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)^{(n_{1})}\coloneqq\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)-\sum_{i=1}^{n_{1}}(-1)^{i-1}q^{i(i-1)/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}\\
\dim{L_{1}^{\prime}/L_{1}}=i\end{subarray}}\partial\mathrm{Den}(L^{\prime}_{1}\oplus L_{2}).

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
be as in Lemma
5.1
, and
\epsilon=\chi(L)
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)^{(n_{1})}=\frac{1}{\alpha(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},I_{n}^{-\epsilon})}\left(2\beta^{\prime}(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},L)^{(n_{1})}+\sum_{i}c^{n,i}_{\epsilon}\beta(\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon},L)^{(n_{1})}\right).

As a corollary of Lemma
5.1
, we have the following.

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
be as in Lemma
5.1
. Then we have the following identity where the summation is finite:

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=\sum_{L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}}\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}.

We may reduce the identity
\mathrm{Int}(L)=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)
to a primitive version as the following theorem shows.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\subset\mathbb{V}
be as in Lemma
5.1
.

(1)  Conjecture
1.1
is true for
L
if for every
L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}
, we have

\mathrm{Int}(L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}.

(2) If Conjecture
1.1
holds for all lattices
L^{\prime}=L_{1}^{\prime}\oplus L_{2}
of
\mathbb{V}
of rank
n
with
L_{1}\subset L_{1}^{\prime}\subset L_{1,F}
, then

\mathrm{Int}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}.

(3)  For
1\leq n_{1}\leq n
, Conjecture
1.1
is true if and only if for every lattice
L=L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\subset\mathbb{V}
with
\mathrm{rank}(L_{1})=n_{1}
, one has

\mathrm{Int}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})^{(n_{1})}.

Proof.

(1) follows from Lemma
2.16
and Corollary
5.5
. (2) follows from Definitions
2.15
and
5.3
. (3) follows from (1) and (2).
∎

For the rest of this section, we assume that
M
is unimodular of rank
m
with a Gram matrix
\mathrm{Diag}(I_{m-1},\nu)
. To go further with
the calculation of
\alpha(M,L,X)
, we need an induction formula for
\beta(M,L,X)^{(\ell)}
as follows. The proof is essentially the same as that of Corollary
9.11
of
[
KR11
]
, and is left to the reader.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
L=L_{1}\obot L_{2}
, where
L_{j}
is of rank
n_{j}
. Let
C(M^{[k]},L_{1})
be the
\mathrm{U}(M^{[k]})
-orbits of sublattices
M(i)\subset M^{[k]}
such that
M(i)
is isometric to
L_{1}
, and write
C(M^{[k]},L_{1})=\sqcup_{i\in J}\{M(i)\}
. Then

\displaystyle\beta(M,L,X)^{(n_{1})}=\sum_{i\in J}|M:M(i)\obot M(i)^{\perp}|^{-n_{2}}|M(i)^{\vee}:M(i)|^{n_{2}}\beta_{i}(M,L_{1},X)\alpha(M(i)^{\perp},L_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\beta_{i}(M,L_{1},X)=\lim_{d\to\infty}q^{-dn_{1}(2m+4k-n_{1})}\#\{\phi\in I(M^{[k]},L_{1},d)^{(n_{1})}\mid\exists\,\ \Phi\in\mathrm{U}(M)\text{ with }\phi(L_{1})=\Phi(M(i))\},

and

\displaystyle I(M^{[k]},L_{1},d)^{(n_{1})}\coloneqq\{\phi\in I(M^{[k]},L_{1},d)\mid\mathrm{rank}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\phi(L_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathbb{F}_{q}=n_{1}\}.

Recall that
I(M^{[k]},L_{1},d)
is defined in (
5.3
).

One special case is that
L=\mathcal{H}^{i}\obot L_{2}
. Since any sublattice of
M^{[k]}=M\obot\mathcal{H}^{k}
isometric to
\mathcal{H}^{i}
is always a direct summand of
M^{[k]}
and
\alpha(M,\mathcal{H}^{i},X)=\beta(M,\mathcal{H}^{i},X)^{(2i)}=\beta(M,\mathcal{H}^{i},X)
, the above proposition specializes as follows.

Corollary 5.8
.

Assume
L=\mathcal{H}^{i}\obot L_{2}
, then

(5.8)

\displaystyle\alpha(M,L,X)=\beta(M,\mathcal{H}^{i},X)\alpha(M,L_{2},q^{2i}X)=\alpha(M,\mathcal{H}^{i},X)\alpha(M,L_{2},q^{2i}X).

We end this section with two more special cases of Proposition
5.7
. Proofs are given in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let the notation be as in Proposition
5.7
. Assume
n_{1}=1
and
L_{1}=\langle t\rangle
where
t\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}.

(1)

There always exists a primitive vector
M(1)\in\mathcal{H}^{k}
with
q(M(1))=t
, and

\displaystyle M(1)^{\perp}\cong\mathcal{H}^{k-1}\obot I_{m}^{\chi(M)}\obot\langle-t\rangle.

Here
\langle t\rangle
denotes a lattice
\mathcal{O}_{F}v
of rank one with
(v,v)=t
.

(2)

If
\mathrm{v}(t)=0
, then there exist a primitive vector
M(0)\in M
with
q(M(0))=t
, and

\displaystyle M(0)^{\perp}\cong\mathcal{H}^{k}\obot I_{m-2}^{\epsilon_{m-2}}\obot\langle\nu t\rangle.

Here
\epsilon_{m-2}=\chi((-1)^{(m-2)(m-3)/2})
.

(3)

If
\mathrm{v}(t)>0
, then there exist a primitive vector
M(0)\in M
with
q(M(0))=t
only when
M
is isotropic (i.e.
\exists\,v\in M
with
q(v)=0
). In this case,

\displaystyle M(0)^{\perp}\cong\mathcal{H}^{k}\obot I_{m-2}^{\chi(M)}\obot\langle-t\rangle.

Assuming the existence of
M(1)
and
M(0)
, we have

\displaystyle|M^{[k]}:M(i)\obot M(i)^{\perp}|^{-1}|M(i)^{\vee}:M(i)|=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $i=1$},\\
q&\text{ if $i=0$}.\end{cases}

(4)

Under the action of
\mathrm{U}(M^{[k]})
,
v
is either in the same orbit of a fixed vector
M(1)\in\mathcal{H}^{k}
or a fixed vector
M(0)\in M
.

(5)

We have the following induction formula:

\displaystyle\beta(M,L,X)^{(1)}=\beta_{1}(M,L_{1},X)\alpha(M(1)^{\perp},L_{2})+q^{n-1}\beta_{0}(M,L_{1},X)\alpha(M(0)^{\perp},L_{2}).

Moreover,

(a)

for any
L_{1}
,

\beta_{1}(M,L_{1},X)=1-X.

(b)

assume
\mathrm{v}(t)=0
, then

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(M,L_{1},X)=\begin{cases}(1+\chi(M)\chi(L)q^{-\frac{m-1}{2}})X&\text{ if $m$ is odd},\\
(1-\chi(M)q^{-\frac{m}{2}})X&\text{ if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

(c)

assume
\mathrm{v}(t)>0
, then

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(M,L_{1},X)=\begin{cases}(1-q^{1-m})X&\text{ if $m$ is odd},\\
\left(1-q^{1-m}+\chi(M)(q-1)q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\right)X&\text{ if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Parts (1)—(4) are proved in subsection
A.1
. The induction formula for
\beta(M,L,X)^{(1)}
follows from Proposition
5.7
. For the formula of
\beta_{i}(M,L_{1},X)
, see Corollaries
A.10
and
A.12
.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let the notation be as in Proposition
5.7
. Assume
\mathrm{v}(L_{1})>0
and
n_{1}=2
. Then we have a partition of
C(M^{[k]},L_{1})=\bigsqcup_{i=0}^{2}C_{i}(M^{[k]},L_{1})
such that for any
M(i)\in C_{i}(M^{[k]},L_{1})
,
M(i)^{\bot}
is isometric to

(-L_{1})\obot\mathcal{H}^{k-i}\obot M^{(i)}.

Here
M^{(i)}
is a unimodular
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice of rank
m-2(2-i)
and has determinant
(-1)^{i}\det L
.

Moreover, we have

(5.9)

\displaystyle\beta(M,L,X)^{(2)}=\sum_{i=0}^{2}q^{(2-i)(n-2)}\beta_{i}(M,L_{1},X)\alpha(M(i)^{\perp},L_{2},X),

where

\displaystyle\beta_{2}(M,L_{1},X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)(1-q^{2}X),

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(M,L_{1},X)

\displaystyle=q(q+1)\left((1-q^{1-m})+\delta_{e}(m)\chi(M)(q-1)q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\right)X(1-X),

\displaystyle\beta_{0}(M,L_{1},X)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}q(1-q^{1-m})(1-q^{3-m})X^{2}&\text{if $m$ is odd},\\
q\left((1-q^{2-m})+\chi(M)(q^{2}-1)q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\right)(1-q^{2-m})X^{2}&\text{if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

Here
\delta_{e}(m)=1
or
0
depending on whether
m
is even or odd.

Proof.

Equation (
5.9
) follows from Proposition
5.7
and Proposition
A.5
. For the formula of
\beta_{i}(S,L_{1},X)
, see Corollaries
A.10
,
A.13
and Proposition
A.14
.
∎

6.
The modified Kudla-Rapoport conjecture

Recall that the Hermitian lattices used to define the correction terms are of the following forms:

(6.1)

\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{i}\obot I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon}\quad\text{ for }1\leq i\leq\frac{n}{2},\quad\epsilon=\pm 1

where
I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon}
is the unimodular Hermitian lattice of rank
n-2i
with

\chi(I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon})=\chi(\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon})=\epsilon
. When
n=2r
is even, we take
I_{0}^{\epsilon}=0
and
\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{1}=\mathcal{H}^{r}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
r_{\epsilon}=\frac{n-1}{2}
when
n
is odd, and
r_{\epsilon}=\lfloor\frac{n+\epsilon}{2}\rfloor
when
n
is even. In the following we just write
r_{\epsilon}
as
r
:

A^{\epsilon}=(A_{i,j}^{\epsilon})=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,1}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,1}^{\epsilon})&\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,2}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,1}^{\epsilon})&\cdots&\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,1}^{\epsilon})\\
0&\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,2}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,2}^{\epsilon})&\cdots&\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,2}^{\epsilon})\\
\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\\
0&0&0&\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{\epsilon})\end{pmatrix},

B^{\epsilon}={}^{t}(\alpha^{\prime}(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{\epsilon}^{n,1}),\cdots,\alpha^{\prime}(I_{n}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,r}^{\epsilon})),

and

C^{\epsilon}={}^{t}(c_{\epsilon}^{n,1},\cdots,c_{\epsilon}^{n,r}),

where
c_{n,i}^{\epsilon}
is as in Conjecture
1.1
.

Then
C^{\epsilon}
is the solution of the equation

(6.2)

A^{\epsilon}C^{\epsilon}=-2B^{\epsilon}.

Moreover,

(6.3)

\displaystyle A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}=2q^{\frac{(n-2j)(n-2j-1)}{2}}\prod_{0<s\leq j}(1-q^{-2s})\prod_{1\leq s\leq\lfloor\frac{n-2j-1}{2}\rfloor}(1-q^{-2s})\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{n-2j}{2}}&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

Finally, for
i<j
,

(6.4)

\displaystyle A_{i,j}^{\epsilon}=A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}\cdot\begin{cases}I(n-2i,\frac{n-2i-1}{2},j-i)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
I(n-2i,\frac{n-2i-1+\epsilon}{2},j-i)&\text{ if $n$ is even},\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle I(n,d,k)\coloneqq\prod_{s=1}^{k}\frac{(q^{d-s+1}-1)(q^{n-d-s}+1)}{q^{s}-1}.

Proof.

First notice that
\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon})=0
if
i<j
. Thus, (
1.9
) is indeed equivalent to (
6.2
), and there exists a unique solution
C_{\epsilon}
.

Now we compute
A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}
explicitly. Corollary
5.8
and Lemma
A.9
imply that

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{j},\mathcal{H}^{j})\alpha(I_{n-2j}^{\epsilon},I_{n-2j}^{\epsilon}).

According to Lemma
A.8
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{j},\mathcal{H}^{j})=\prod_{0<s\leq j}(1-q^{-2s}).

By Lemma
A.11
,

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{n-2j}^{\epsilon},I_{n-2j}^{\epsilon})=|\mathrm{O}(\overline{I}_{n-2j}^{\epsilon})(\mathbb{F}_{q})|,

where
\overline{I}_{n-2j}^{\epsilon}=I_{n-2j}^{\epsilon}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{F}/(\pi)
is the space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
with the naturally induced quadratic form.
Now (
6.3
) follows from the well-known formula:

\displaystyle|\mathrm{O}(\overline{I}_{n-2j}^{\epsilon})(\mathbb{F}_{q})|=\begin{cases}2q^{\frac{(n-2j)(n-2j-1)}{2}}\prod_{s=1}^{\frac{n-2j-1}{2}}(1-q^{-2s})&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
2q^{\frac{(n-2j)(n-2j-1)}{2}}(1-\epsilon q^{-\frac{n-2j}{2}})\prod_{s=1}^{\frac{n-2j}{2}-1}(1-q^{-2s})&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

To obtain (
6.4
), notice that
(Corollary
5.8
)

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon})=\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}^{i})\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon},I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon}),

and

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon})=\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}^{i})\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon}).

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{A_{i,j}^{\epsilon}}{A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}}=\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,i}^{\epsilon})}{\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n,j}^{\epsilon})}=\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon},I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon})}{\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon})}.

Fix an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice
L
that is represented by
I_{n-2i}^{\epsilon}
. According to Lemma
6.2
, to compute
\frac{A_{i,j}^{\epsilon}}{A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}}
, we need to count the number of lattices
L^{\prime}
in
L_{F}
such that contain
L\subset L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime}\cong\mathcal{H}_{n-2i,j-i}^{\epsilon}
, which is equivalent to the following condition:

\displaystyle\pi L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j-i}}{{\subset}}\pi L^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n-2j}}{{\subset}}(L^{\prime})^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j-i}}{{\subset}}L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j-i}}{{\subset}}{L^{\prime}}.

Since
L^{\prime}
and
\pi L^{\prime}
determine each other, we just need to count
\pi L^{\prime}
satisfying the above condition. We regard
\pi L^{\prime}/\pi L
as a
(j-i)
-dimensional subspace of
L/\pi L
, where
L/\pi L
is equipped with quadratic form
(x,y)/\pi
.

Claim: The condition

\displaystyle\pi L^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\subset}}(L^{\prime})^{\sharp}

is equivalent to the condition that
\pi L^{\prime}/\pi L
is an isotropic subspace of
L/\pi L.

Indeed, assume
\pi L^{\prime}/\pi L
is an isotropic subspace of
L/\pi L.
Then
(\pi x,\pi y)\in\pi\mathcal{O}_{F}
for any
x,y\in L^{\prime}
, which is equivalent to
(x,\pi y)\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
for any
x,y\in L^{\prime}
. The latter condition is the same as
L^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\subset}}(L^{\prime})^{\sharp}
. The other direction is clear.

Therefore

\frac{A_{i,j}^{\epsilon}}{A_{j,j}^{\epsilon}}
is the number of
(j-i)
-dimensional isotropic subspaces of
L/\pi L
. According to
[
LZ22b
, Lemma 3.2.2]
, it equals to

\begin{cases}I(n-2i,\frac{n-2i-1}{2},j-i)&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
I(n-2i,\frac{n-2i-1+\epsilon}{2},j-i)&\text{ if $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

∎

According to Theorem
6.1
,
in order to solve
C^{\epsilon}
, we need to know
B^{\epsilon}
and
A^{\epsilon}
. Here,
B^{\epsilon}
can be calculated by applying Corollary
5.8
and Proposition
5.9
inductively. The following lemma can be used to compute
A^{\epsilon}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
F/F_{0}
be a quadratic
p
-adic field extension, and let
L
and
M
be two
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-Hermitian lattices of rank
n
. Then
\frac{\alpha(M,L)}{\alpha(M,M)}
is equal to the number of lattices
L^{\prime}
in
L_{F}
containing
L
and isometric to
M
.

Proof.

The proof is a generalization of that of Proposition 10.2 of
[
KR14b
]
and works for both inert and ramified primes.

Let us assume that there is an isometric embedding from
L
into
M
, otherwise both sides of the identity in the lemma are zero. In this case, we have a fixed
L_{F}\cong M_{F}
.
Let
\alpha
(respectively,
\beta
) be a top degree translation invariant form on
L_{F}^{n}
(respectively,
\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F)
). Let
\nu_{p}=\alpha/h^{*}(\beta)
where

h:\quad L_{F}^{n}\rightarrow\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(F),\quad x\mapsto(x,x).

Define
X
to be the set of
F
-linear isometric embeddings from
L
into
M
. By fixing a basis of
L_{F}
and regarding
\phi\in X
as a linear isometry from
L_{F}
to itself, we identify
X
as a subset of
L_{F}^{n}
.
By the argument in Section 3 of
[
GY00
]
(in particular Lemma 3.4), we know that

(6.5)

\alpha(M,L)=\mathrm{vol}(X,d\nu_{p})\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}),d\beta)}{\mathrm{vol}((M)^{n},d\alpha)}.

For any
\phi\in X
regarded as a linear isometry from
L_{F}
to itself, the lattice
L_{\phi}\coloneqq\phi^{-1}(M)
is a lattice containing
L
. Conversely, for any
L^{\prime}
containing
L
and isometric to
M
, there is a
\phi\in X
such that
L_{\phi}=L^{\prime}
. Hence we have a partition

X=\bigsqcup_{L\subset L^{\prime}}X_{L^{\prime}},\quad X_{L^{\prime}}\coloneqq\{\phi\in X\mid L_{\phi}=L^{\prime}\}.

Since each
L^{\prime}
is isomorphic to
M
, all the
X_{L^{\prime}}
have the same volume as that of
X_{M}
. Specializing (
6.5
) to
L=M
, we see

(6.6)

\alpha(M,M)=\mathrm{vol}(X_{M},d\nu_{p})\frac{\mathrm{vol}(\mathrm{Herm}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F}),d\beta)}{\mathrm{vol}((M)^{n},d\alpha)}.

Dividing equation (
6.5
) by (
6.6
), we prove the lemma.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

When
F/F_{0}
is unramified and
M
is unimodular, the lemma was proved by equation (3.6.1.1) of
[
LZ22a
]
.

Now we specialize Theorem
6.1
to the case
n=3
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume
n=3
and
\epsilon=\chi(L)
. Then
c_{3,1}^{\epsilon}=\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}
, hence

\partial\mathrm{Den}(L)=2\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},L)}{\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},I_{3}^{-\epsilon})}+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon},L)}{\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},I_{3}^{-\epsilon})}.

Proof.

First of all, according to Theorem
6.1
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon})=2(1-q^{-2}).

By Corollary
5.8
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon},X)=\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H},X)\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},I_{1}^{\epsilon},q^{2}X).

According to Lemmas
A.8
and
A.9
,

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H},X)=\beta(\mathcal{H}^{k},\mathcal{H})=1-X.

Lemma
7.1
gives that

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},I_{1}^{\epsilon},q^{2}X)=1-qX.

Hence

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon},X)=(1-X)(1-qX),

and

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(I_{3}^{-\epsilon},\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{\epsilon})=1-q.

Combining this with (
6.7
), we solve (
6.2
) and obtain

\displaystyle c_{3,1}^{\epsilon}=\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}.

Now the lemma follows from (
1.10
).
∎

7.
Local density formula when
\mathrm{rank}(T)\leq 2

The main purpose of this section is to give an explicit formula for
\alpha(I,T,X)
where
I=\mathrm{Diag}(I_{m-1},\nu)
with
\nu\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
and
\mathrm{rank}(T)\leq 2
.

7.1.
The case
T=(t)
.

In order to apply induction formulas to calculate
\alpha(I,T,X)
for
T
with
\hbox{rank}(T)=2
, we need to consider the case
T=(t)
first.
Write
t=t_{0}(-\pi_{0})^{\mathrm{v}(t)}
for
t_{0}\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
, and

(7.1)

I_{a,b}=\mathrm{Diag}(I,\nu_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},\nu_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b})=\mathrm{Diag}(s_{1},\dots,s_{m+2})

for integers
0\leq a\leq b
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Assume
0\leq a\leq b\leq\mathrm{v}(t)
.

(1)

If
m
is odd, then

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)=1

\displaystyle+\chi(I)\chi(-\nu_{1})(q-1)\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}q^{-ms+a+\frac{m-1}{2}}X^{s}

\displaystyle+\chi(I_{a,b})\chi(t_{0})q^{-(m+1)\mathrm{v}(t)+a+b-\frac{m+1}{2}}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}.

(2)

If
m
is even, then

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)=1

\displaystyle+\chi(I)(q-1)\sum_{s=1}^{a}q^{-(m-1)s+\frac{m}{2}-1}X^{s}

\displaystyle+\chi(I_{a,b})q^{a+b}\left((q-1)\sum_{s=b+1}^{\mathrm{v}(t)}q^{-(m+1)s+\frac{m}{2}}X^{s}-q^{-(m+1)\mathrm{v}(t)-1-\frac{m}{2}}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}\right).

Proof.

Direct calculation gives

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)

\displaystyle=\int_{F_{0}}\,dY\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{2k+m+2}}\psi(\langle Y,\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H}^{k},I_{a,b})[{\bf x}]-t\rangle)\,d{\bf x}

\displaystyle=\int_{F_{0}}\psi(-tY)\,dY\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{2k}\times\mathcal{O}_{F}^{m+2}}\psi(Y\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{tr}(\frac{1}{\pi}x_{i}\bar{y}_{i})+Y\sum_{l=1}^{m+2}s_{l}z_{l}\bar{z}_{l})\prod_{i}dx_{i}dy_{i}\prod_{l}dz_{l}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathrm{v}(Y)=-s}I_{k}(Y)I_{I_{a,b}}(Y)\psi(-tY)\,dY.

Here, according to
[
Shi22
, Lemma 7.6]
,

I_{k}(Y)=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{2k}}\psi(Y\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathrm{tr}(\frac{1}{\pi}x_{i}\bar{y}_{i}))\prod dx_{i}dy_{i}=q^{-2ks},

and

I_{I_{a,b}}(Y)=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F}^{m+2}}\psi(Y\sum_{l=1}^{m+2}s_{l}z_{l}\bar{z}_{l})\prod dz_{l}=\prod_{l=1}^{m+2}J(s_{l}Y),

where

(7.2)

J(t)=\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\psi(tz\bar{z})dz=\begin{cases}1&\hbox{ if }\mathrm{v}(t)\geq 0,\\
q^{\mathrm{v}(t)}\chi(-t_{0})g(\chi,\psi_{\frac{1}{\pi_{0}}})&\hbox{ if }\mathrm{v}(t)<0,\end{cases}

and

g(\chi,\psi_{\frac{1}{\pi_{0}}})=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}/\pi_{0}}\chi(x)\psi(\frac{x}{\pi_{0}})

is the Gauss sum. Write
\psi^{\prime}=\psi_{\frac{1}{\pi_{0}}}
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{s=1}^{a}q^{s}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}}q^{-2ks}\cdot q^{-ms}\chi(\nu(-Y)^{m})g(\chi,\psi^{\prime})^{m}\psi(-(-\pi_{0})^{s}Yt)dY

\displaystyle+\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}}q^{-2ks}\cdot q^{-ms+a}\chi(\nu_{1}\nu(-Y)^{m+1})g(\chi,\psi^{\prime})^{m+1}\psi(-(-\pi_{0})^{-s}Yt)\,dY

\displaystyle+\sum_{s=b+1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}}q^{-2ks}\cdot q^{-(m+1)s+a+b}\chi(\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu(-Y)^{m+2})g(\chi,\psi^{\prime})^{m+2}\psi(-(-\pi_{0})^{-s}Yt)\,dY.

Recall the well-known facts that

\displaystyle g(\chi,\psi^{\prime})^{2}

\displaystyle=\chi(-1)\cdot q,

(7.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}}\psi((-\pi_{0})^{-s}Yt)dY

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Char}(\pi_{0}^{s}\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}})(t)-q^{-1}\operatorname{Char}(\pi_{0}^{s-1}\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}})(t),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}}\chi(Y)\psi((-\pi_{0})^{-s}Yt)dY

\displaystyle=\chi(-t_{0})q^{-1}g(\chi,\psi^{\prime})\operatorname{Char}(\pi_{0}^{s-1}\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times})(t).

When
m
is odd, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)=1

\displaystyle+\chi((-1)^{\frac{m+1}{2}}\nu_{1}\nu)(q-1)\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}q^{-ms+a+\frac{m-1}{2}}X^{s}

\displaystyle+\chi((-1)^{\frac{m+1}{2}}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu t_{0})q^{-(m+1)(\mathrm{v}(t)+1)+a+b+\frac{m+1}{2}}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}.

When
m
is even, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(I_{a,b},(t),X)

\displaystyle=1+\chi((-1)^{\frac{m}{2}}\nu)(q-1)\sum_{s=1}^{a}q^{-(m-1)s+\frac{m}{2}-1}X^{s}+\chi((-1)^{\frac{m+2}{2}}\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\nu)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left((q-1)\sum_{s=b+1}^{\mathrm{v}(t)}q^{-(m+1)s+a+b+\frac{m}{2}}X^{s}-q^{-(m+1)(\mathrm{v}(t)+1)+a+b+\frac{m}{2}}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}\right).

Finally, notice that for
I
of rank
m
we have

\chi(I)=\begin{cases}\chi((-1)^{\frac{m-1}{2}}\nu)&\text{ if $m$ is odd},\\
\chi((-1)^{\frac{m}{2}}\nu)&\text{ if $m$ is even}.\end{cases}

Now the lemma is clear.
∎

Similarly, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
I
be unimodular with odd rank
m
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha(I\obot\mathcal{H}_{i},(t),X)=\begin{cases}1+\chi(I)\chi(t_{0})q^{-(\mathrm{v}(t)+1)(m+1)+\frac{m+1}{2}+i}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}&\text{ if $i\leq 2\mathrm{v}(t)$},\\
1+\chi(I)\chi(t_{0})q^{-(\mathrm{v}(t)+1)(m-1)+\frac{m-1}{2}}X^{\mathrm{v}(t)+1}&\text{ if $i>2\mathrm{v}(t)$}.\end{cases}

7.2.
The case
T=\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b})

In this subsection, we compute
\alpha(I,T,X)
for
I
unimodular of rank
m\geq 2
and
T=\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b})
with
0\leq a\leq b
. Notice that
\alpha(I,T,X)=0
when
a<0
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Assume
T=\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b})
and that
I
is isotropic of even rank
m\geq 2
, then

\displaystyle\alpha(I,T,X)=

\displaystyle(1-X)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}+\gamma_{e}(I,T,X)\right)

\displaystyle+qX(q^{2-m}X)^{a}(1-\chi(I)q^{-\frac{m}{2}})(1+\chi(I)\chi(T)q^{\frac{m-2}{2}}(q^{2-m}X)^{b+1})

\displaystyle+\left(1-q^{-(m-1)}+(q-1)\chi(I)q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\right)X

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\left(q\sum_{i=0}^{a-1}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}+\gamma_{e}(I,T,X)-\chi(I)\chi(T)q^{\frac{m}{2}}(q^{2-m}X)^{a+b+1}\right),

where

\displaystyle\gamma_{e}(I,T,X)=\chi(I)q^{\frac{m}{2}}\left(\sum_{d=1}^{a}(q^{d}-1)(q^{2-m}X)^{d}+\chi(T)q^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{b+1}\sum_{i=0}^{a}(q^{1-m}X)^{i}\right).

Proof.

Since
I
is of even rank,
u_{1}\cdot I^{[k]}\approx I^{[k]}
, and we may assume
T
is of the form
\mathrm{Diag}((-\pi_{0})^{a},u(-\pi_{0})^{b})
without loss of generality.

According to Theorem
5.2
and Proposition
5.9
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(I,T,X)

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}(I,(-\pi_{0})^{a},X)\alpha(M(1)^{\perp},u(-\pi_{0})^{b})

\displaystyle\quad+q\beta_{0}(I,(-\pi_{0})^{a},X)\alpha(M(0)^{\perp},u(-\pi_{0})^{b})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{2-m}X\alpha(I,\mathrm{Diag}((-\pi_{0})^{a-1},u(-\pi_{0})^{b}),X)

where
M(1)^{\perp}=\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H}^{k-1},-(-\pi_{0})^{i},I)
and

M(0)^{\perp}=\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H}^{k},\underbrace{-(-\pi_{0})^{i},1,\dots,1,-\nu}_{m-1}).

Continuing this process, we obtain

\displaystyle\alpha(I,T,X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{a-i}\cdot\left(\beta_{1}(I,(-\pi_{0})^{i},X)\alpha(M(1)^{\perp},u(-\pi_{0})^{b})+q\beta_{0}(I,(-\pi_{0})^{i},X)\alpha(M(0)^{\perp},u(-\pi_{0})^{b})\right).

By the formulas in Proposition
5.9
and Lemma
7.1
, the above is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{a-i}(1-X)

\displaystyle\cdot\bigg(1+(q-1)\chi(I)q^{\frac{m-2}{2}}\sum_{s=-i}^{-1}(q^{(m-1)}(q^{2}X)^{-1})^{s}+\chi(I)\chi(T)q^{i+\frac{m}{2}}(q^{2-m}X)^{b+1}\bigg)

\displaystyle+q(q^{2-m}X)^{a}X(1-q^{-\frac{m}{2}}\chi(I))(1+\chi(I)\chi(T)q^{-(b+1)(m-2)+\frac{m-2}{2}}X^{b+1})

\displaystyle+q\sum_{i=1}^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{a-i}X\left((1-q^{-(m-1)})+(q-1)\chi(I)q^{-(m-1)+\frac{m-2}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\left(1+(q-1)\chi(I)q^{\frac{m-4}{2}}\sum_{s=-i}^{-1}(q^{(m-3)}X^{-1})^{s}+\chi(I)\chi(T)q^{i+\frac{m-2}{2}}(q^{2-m}X)^{b+1}\right).

Now the transformation

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{a}\sum_{s=1}^{i}q^{s}(q^{2-m}X)^{a-i+s}=\sum_{d=1}^{a}\sum_{s=1}^{d}q^{s}(q^{2-m}X)^{d}

and some calculation gives us the result we want.
∎

The case that
I
is anisotropic (i.e. when
m=2
and
\chi(I)=-1
) can be computed similarly and is simpler. We omit the detail here. In particular, we may recover the following formula.

Proposition 7.4
.

[
Shi22
, Theorem 6.2(1)]

Assume
I=\mathrm{Diag}(1,\nu)
, then

\displaystyle\alpha(I,T,X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)(1+\chi(I)+q\chi(I))\sum_{e=0}^{\alpha}(qX)^{e}-\chi(T)q^{\alpha+1}X^{\beta+1}(1-X)\sum_{e=0}^{\alpha}(q^{-1}X)^{e}

\displaystyle\quad-\chi(T)(1+q)(X^{\alpha+\beta+2}+\chi(I)\chi(T))+(1+\chi(I))q^{\alpha+1}X^{\alpha+1}(1+\chi(T)X^{\beta-\alpha}).

Moreover, a similar computation yields the following, and we leave the detail to reader.

Proposition 7.5
.

Assume that
I
is unimodular of odd rank
m\geq 3
. Then

\displaystyle\alpha(I,T,X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)\left(\sum_{i=0}^{a}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}+\gamma_{o,1}(I,T,X)\right)+(1-q^{-(m-1)})X\left(q\sum_{i=0}^{a-1}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}+\gamma_{o,0}(S,T,X)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+qX(q^{2-m}X)^{a}\left(1+\chi(I)\chi(u_{1})q^{-\frac{m-1}{2}}\right)\left(1-\chi(I)\chi(u_{1})q^{(2-m)b-\frac{m-1}{2}}X^{b+1}\right),

where
\gamma_{o,1}(I,T,X)
equals

\displaystyle\chi(I)\chi(u_{1})q^{\frac{m-1}{2}}\left(\sum_{d=a+1}^{a+b}(q^{a+b+1-d}-1)(q^{2-m}X)^{d}-\sum_{i=b+1}^{a+b+1}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}\right),

and
\gamma_{o,0}(I,T,X)
equals

\displaystyle\chi(I)\chi(u_{1})q^{\frac{m-1}{2}}\left(\sum_{d=a+1}^{a+b}(q^{a+b+1-d}-q)(q^{2-m}X)^{d}-\sum_{i=b+1}^{a+b}(q^{2-m}X)^{i}\right).

8.
Local density formula when
\mathrm{rank}(T)=3

In this section, we always assume
\mathrm{rank}(T)=3
and
S=I_{3}^{-\chi(T)}
. The aim of this section is to compute
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)
explicitly. We treat the case
\mathrm{v}(T)\leq-1
in the first subsection. In the second subsection, we deal with the case when
T=\mathrm{Diag}(1,T_{2})
for
T_{2}
diagonal. In the last subsection, instead of
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)
, we compute
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}
for
T
of the form not covered by previous subsections.

8.1.
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)
for
T
with
\mathrm{v}(T)\leq-1

Proposition 8.1
.

If
\mathrm{v}(T)\leq-1
, then
\mathrm{Int}(T)=\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)=0
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{v}(T)<-1
, then
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)=0
since
\mathrm{v}(S^{[k]})\geq-1
where
S^{[k]}=S\obot\mathcal{H}^{k}
. If
\mathrm{v}(T)=-1
, then
T
is of the form
\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H},(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c}))
with
\chi(T)=\chi(u_{3})
. In this case, according to Corollary
5.8
, Lemmas
A.8
and
A.9
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(S,T,X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)\alpha(S,(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c}),q^{2}X).

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)

\displaystyle=\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},\mathcal{H})\alpha(u_{3},(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c}))

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-2})\alpha(u_{3},(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c})).

Hence, applying Lemma
7.1
to
I_{0,0}=S
where
I
is of rank 1, we have

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)

\displaystyle=2\alpha(S,(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c}),q^{2})+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}(1-q^{-2})\alpha((u_{3}),(u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c}))

\displaystyle=2(1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q)+2(q-1)

\displaystyle=2(1-q)+2(q-1)

\displaystyle=0.

Here we are using the fact
\chi(S)\chi(T)=\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})=-1
.
∎

8.2.
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)
for
T=\mathrm{Diag}(1,T_{2})
with
T_{2}
diagonal

In this subsection, we assume
T=\mathrm{Diag}(1,T_{2})
, where
T_{2}=\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b})
with
0\leq a\leq b
. Let
u=u_{1}u_{2}
. In addition, let
S=\mathrm{Diag}(1,1,\nu)
and
S_{2}=\mathrm{Diag}(1,\nu)
. We compare
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)
and
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T_{2})
in this subsection.

Recall that

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(S,T)}{\alpha(S,S)}+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)}{\alpha(S,S)}.

Moreover, according to
[
Shi22
, Theorem 1.3]
and
[
HSY23
, Theorem 1.1]
, the analytic side in the case
n=2
is

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T_{2})

\displaystyle=2\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(S_{2},T_{2})}{\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})}-\frac{2q^{2}}{q^{2}-1}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H},T_{2})}{\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})}.

Proposition 8.2
.

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(T_{2})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1+2\sum_{i=1}^{a}q^{i}&\text{ if $\chi(T)=1$},\\
1&\text{ if $\chi(T)=-1$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Proposition
5.9
implies that
\alpha(S,T,X)
equals

\displaystyle(1-X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,S),T_{2},q^{2}X)+q^{2}(1+q^{-1}\chi(S))X\alpha(S_{2},T_{2},X).

Hence

(8.1)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(S,T)=\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,S),T_{2},q^{2})+q^{2}(1+q^{-1}\chi(S))\alpha^{\prime}(S_{2},T_{2}).

According to Lemma
A.11
, one can check that
\alpha(S,S)=\beta(S,S)=2q(q^{2}-1)
, and
\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})=2(q-\chi(S_{2}))
. Then

(8.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(S,T)}{\alpha(S,S)}-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}(S_{2},T_{2})}{\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})}=\frac{\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,S),T_{2},q^{2})}{\alpha(S,S)}.

Hence we just need to check that

\displaystyle 2\frac{\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,S),T_{2},q^{2})}{\alpha(S,S)}+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q^{2}}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)}{\alpha(S,S)}+\frac{2q^{2}}{q^{2}-1}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H},T_{2})}{\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1+2\sum_{i=1}^{a}q^{i}&\text{ if $\chi(T)=1$},\\
1&\text{ if $\chi(T)=-1$}.\end{cases}

By Proposition
7.3
, we may check that

(8.3)

\displaystyle 2\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,S),T_{2},q^{2})=\begin{cases}2(2q^{a+2}-(q+1)^{2})(q-1)&\text{ if $\chi(T)=1$},\\
2(q-1)(q^{2}-1)&\text{ if $\chi(T)=-1$}.\end{cases}

To compute
\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)
, we may choose
\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)}=\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H},1)
when
\chi(T)=1
.
By Corollary
5.8
, Proposition
7.4
, and a direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)}{\alpha(S,S)}=\frac{1}{2q(q^{2}-1)}\cdot\begin{cases}(q-1)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,1),T_{2})+\frac{2q^{2}}{q-1}\alpha(\mathcal{H},T_{2})&\text{ if $\chi(T)=1$}\\
(q-1)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-1,-u),T_{2})&\text{ if $\chi(T)=-1$}.\end{cases}

Combining this with the formulas in
[
HSY23
, Theorem 6.1]
, we have

(8.4)

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2}}{1+q^{2}}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)}{\alpha(S,S)}+\frac{2q^{2}}{q^{2}-1}\frac{\alpha(\mathcal{H},T_{2})}{\alpha(S_{2},S_{2})}=\frac{1}{q(q^{2}-1)}\cdot\begin{cases}4q^{a+2}-q^{2}-2q-1&\text{ if $\chi(T)=1$}\\
(q^{2}-1)&\text{ if $\chi(T)=-1$}.\end{cases}

Now a direct computation combined with (
8.3
) and (
8.4
) proves the proposition.
∎

Corollary 8.3
.

Assume
L
is a Hermitian lattice with Gram matrix
T
, then

(8.5)

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)-\partial\mathrm{Den}(T_{2})=|\{\mathcal{V}^{0}(L)\}|.

Proof.

We can write
L=L^{\flat}\obot\mathcal{O}_{F}{\bf x}
where
q({\bf x})=1
. If
L^{\flat}
is non-split, then
|\{\mathcal{V}^{0}(L)\}|=1
.

If
L^{\flat}
is split, then
|\{\mathcal{V}^{0}(L)\}|=1+2\sum_{i=1}^{a}q^{i}
since
\mathcal{L}_{3}(L)
can be identified with
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}(L^{\flat})
, which is a ball in
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
centered at a vertex lattice of type
0
with radius
a
(see
[
HSY23
]
for more detail). Here
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
is the Bruhat-Tits tree associated with
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,1}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}(L^{\flat})
is the subtree of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
associated with
L^{\flat}
.
∎

8.3.
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}

In this subsection, we assume
T=\mathrm{Diag}(T_{2},u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c})
with
\mathrm{v}(T_{2})>0
, and compute
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}
. Recall that
\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}=\partial\mathrm{Den}(L^{\flat}\obot\mathcal{O}_{F}{\bf x})^{(2)}
where the Gram matrix of
L=L^{\flat}\obot\mathcal{O}_{F}{\bf x}
is
T
. We consider two cases separately in Propositions
8.4
and
8.5
.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
T=\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1}(-\pi_{0})^{a},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{b},u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c})
where
0<a\leq b\leq c
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}=1+\chi(-u_{2}u_{3})q^{a}(q^{a}-q^{b})-q^{a+b}.

Proof.

Recall that

\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2q(q^{2}-1)}\left(2\beta^{\prime}(S,T)^{(2)}+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)^{(2)}\right).

We compute
\beta^{\prime}(S,T)^{(2)}
first. According to Proposition
5.10
,
\beta_{0}(S,T_{2},X)=0
and

\displaystyle\beta(S,T,X)^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}(S,T_{2},X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)

\displaystyle\quad+q\beta_{1}(S,T_{2},X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)(1-q^{2}X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)

\displaystyle\quad+(q+1)(q^{2}-1)X(1-X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X).

According to Lemma
7.1
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)=1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{1})(q-1)\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}q^{a+1}(qX)^{s}+\chi(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\nu)q^{a+b+2}X^{c+1},

and

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,-T_{2}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X)=1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{1})(q-1)q^{a}\sum_{s=a+1}^{b}(qX)^{s}+\chi(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\nu)q^{a+b+1}X^{c+1}.

The relation
\chi(u_{1}u_{2}u_{3}\nu)=\chi(S)\chi(T)=-1
and a direct calculation show that

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(S,T_{2})^{(2)}

\displaystyle=1+\chi(-u_{2}u_{3})q^{a}(q^{a}-q^{b})-q^{a+b}.

Finally,
\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)^{(2)}=0
by Proposition
5.10
. The proposition is proved.
∎

Proposition 8.5
.

Recall that
\mathcal{H}_{a}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\pi^{a}\\
(-\pi)^{a}&0\end{pmatrix}
. Let
T=\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H}_{a},u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c})
where
a
is a positive odd integer and
c\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}=\begin{cases}(1-q^{a})&\text{ if $a\leq 2c$},\\
(1-q^{2c+1})&\text{ if $a>2c$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that

\partial\mathrm{Den}(T)^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2q(q^{2}-1)}\left(2\beta^{\prime}(S,T)^{(2)}+\frac{q^{2}}{1+q}\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)^{(2)}\right).

We need to compute
\beta^{\prime}(S,T)^{(2)}
and
\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)^{(2)}
.

According to Proposition
5.10
,
\beta_{0}(S,T_{2},X)=0
and

\displaystyle\beta(S,T,X)^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\beta_{2}(S,\mathcal{H}_{a},X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)

\displaystyle\quad+q\beta_{1}(S,\mathcal{H}_{a},X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X)

\displaystyle=(1-X)\Big((1-q^{2}X)\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)

\displaystyle\quad\,+(q+1)(q^{2}-1)X\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X)\Big).

According to Lemma
7.2
,

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(S,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{4}X)=\begin{cases}1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{2+a}X^{c+1}&\text{ if $a\leq 2c$},\\
1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{2c+3}X^{c+1}&\text{ if $a>2c$},\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathrm{Diag}(-\nu,\mathcal{H}_{a}),u_{3}(-\pi_{0})^{c},q^{2}X)=\begin{cases}1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{1+a}X^{c+1}&\text{ if $a\leq 2c$},\\
1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{2c+2}X^{c+1}&\text{ if $a>2c$}.\end{cases}

A short computation shows that

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(S,T)^{(2)}=q(q^{2}-1)\cdot\begin{cases}1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{a}&\text{ if $a\leq 2c$},\\
1+\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})q^{2c+1}&\text{ if $a>2c$}.\end{cases}

Notice that
\chi(S)\chi(u_{3})=\chi(S)\chi(T)=-1
. Finally,
\beta(\mathcal{H}^{3,1}_{\chi(T)},T)^{(2)}=0
by Proposition
5.10
. The proposition is proved.
∎

Part III
Proof of the main theorem

9.
Reduced locus of special cycle

As remarked in §
2
, results of
[
RTW14
]
extend to the category of strict formal
\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}
-modules using relative Dieudonné theory.

9.1.
The Bruhat-Tits building for
n=3

From now on we assume
n=3
and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{3}
as in Section
2.3
.

Lemma 9.1
.

(1)

For every
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}
is isomorphic to the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
k
. Its
q+1
rational points correspond to all
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
contained in
\Lambda_{2}
.

(2)

Every
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
is contained in
q+1
type
2
lattices. In other words, there are
q+1
projective lines in
(\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathrm{Pap}})_{\mathrm{red}}
passing through the superspecial point
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)
. Moreover

(9.1)

\bigcap_{\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{2},\,\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda_{2}}\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}=\pi\Lambda_{0}.

Proof.

Suppose
z\in\mathcal{N}(k)
and
M\coloneqq M(z)\subset N
is defined as in Proposition
2.9
. Since
n=3
, by
[
RTW14
, Proposition 4.1]
we have
\Lambda(M)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}=M+\tau(M)
.

Proof of (1):
Suppose
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)
, i.e.
M\subset\Lambda_{2}
.

If
M=\tau(M)
, then
M=\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
for some
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
contained in
\Lambda_{2}
.

If
M\neq\tau(M)
, then by taking the dual of
M\subset\Lambda_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
we have the following sequence of inclusions

(9.2)

(\Lambda_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\subset}}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\subset}}M+\tau(M)=\Lambda_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}.

In both cases the class of
M
in
\Lambda_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}/(\Lambda_{2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})^{\sharp}\cong k^{2}
is a line. This finishes the proof of (1).

Proof of (2): For each
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
we just need to count the number of lattices
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
that contains
\Lambda_{0}
. We have the following sequence of inclusions

\pi\Lambda_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{\subset}}\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\subset}}\Lambda_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\subset}}\Lambda_{2}.

With respect to the quadratic form
(\,,)\pmod{\pi}
on
\Lambda_{0}/\pi\Lambda_{0}
, the dual lattice
\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}
corresponds to the
2
-dimensional subspaces
U\coloneqq\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}/\pi\Lambda_{0}
in
\Lambda_{0}/\pi\Lambda_{0}
such that
U^{\bot}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\subset}}U
. Thus, we just need to count the number of isotropic lines
U^{\bot}
. Assume that
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
is a basis of
\Lambda_{0}/\pi\Lambda_{0}
whose Gram matrix with respect to the quadratic form
(\,,)_{\mathbb{X}}\pmod{\pi}
is

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}&1&\\
1&&\\
&&\epsilon\end{array}\right).

It is easy to see that the isotropic lines are
\mathrm{Span}\{e_{1}\}
,
\mathrm{Span}\{e_{2}\}
and
\mathrm{Span}\{e_{1}-\frac{\epsilon a^{2}}{2}e_{2}+ae_{3}\}
(
a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
). Finally,
equation (
9.1
) can be checked directly using this basis.
∎

It is well known that
\mathcal{L}
is a tree, see for example
[
Bro89
, Section 3 of Chapter VI]
. More specifically, the vertices of
\mathcal{L}
correspond to vertex lattices of type
2
or
0
. There is an edge between
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
and
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
if
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
. We give each edge length
\frac{1}{2}
. This defines a metric
d(\,,)
on
\mathcal{L}
. Recall that we have defined
\mathcal{L}(L)
in (
2.6
).
Then the boundary of
\mathcal{L}(L)
is the set

(9.3)

\mathcal{B}(L)=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L)\mid\exists\,\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}\text{ such that }\Lambda\subset\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{2}\notin\mathcal{L}(L)\}.

Recall we have the isomorphism
b:\mathbb{V}\rightarrow C
defined in (
2.3
).
Recall from
[
KR14a
]
or
[
HSY23
]
that the vertices of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
correspond to vertex lattices of type
2
, and an edge corresponds to a vertex lattice of type
0
. Each vertex of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
is contained in
q+1
edges and each edge connects exactly two vertices.
For
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}
with
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})=0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}\}^{\perp}
split, recall that
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
is the Bruhat-Tits tree of
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,1}
.
Then
{\bf x}
determines an embedding
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{L}
defined as follows. First we send each vertex of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
corresponding to a vertex lattice
\Lambda\subset\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{b({\bf x})\}^{\perp}
of type
2
to the vertex of
\mathcal{L}
corresponding to the type
2
lattice
\Lambda\obot\mathrm{Span}\{b({\bf x})\}
. An edge of
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
corresponding to a type 0 lattice
\Lambda_{0}\subset\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{b({\bf x})\}^{\perp}
is broken into two pieces evenly and sent to the union of the two edges in
\mathcal{L}
joining the two vertices corresponding to
\Lambda\obot\mathrm{Span}\{b({\bf x})\}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}\obot\mathrm{Span}\{b({\bf x})\}
where
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
are the two type
2
lattices containing
\Lambda_{0}
.

9.2.
Rank
1
case

Lemma 9.2
.

A point
z\in\mathcal{N}_{3}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(k)
is in
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
if and only if
b({\bf x})\in M(z)
.

(1)

Assume
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
, then the superspecial point
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)
is in
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
if and only if
b({\bf x})\in\Lambda_{0}
.

(2)

Assume
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)&\text{ if }b({\bf x})\in\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp},\\
\text{a superspecial point in }\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)&\text{ if }b({\bf x})\in\Lambda_{2}\backslash\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp},\\
\emptyset&\text{ if }b({\bf x})\notin\Lambda_{2}.\end{cases}

Proof.

By Dieudonné theory,
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
if and only if
{\bf x}(M(\mathbb{Y}))\subset M(z)
if and only if
b({\bf x})\in M(z)
since
e
is a generator of
M(\mathbb{Y})
. For
z=\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)
where
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
, we have
M(z)=\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
. Hence (1) immediately follows.

Now we proceed to prove (2).
If
b({\bf x})\in\Lambda^{\sharp}
, then (
9.2
) tells us that
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
for any
z\in\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)
. The fact that
\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}\subset\Lambda_{0}
for any
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
contained in
\Lambda_{2}
implies that
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
. So
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
.

If
b({\bf x})\in\Lambda_{2}\backslash\Lambda_{2}^{\sharp}
, then
\Lambda_{0}\coloneqq\Lambda^{\sharp}+\mathrm{Span}\{b({\bf x})\}
is a type
0
lattice contained in
\Lambda_{2}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)
. On the other hand, since
\tau(\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}})=\Lambda_{0}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\mathcal{O}_{\breve{F}}
, equation (
9.2
) tells us that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})
does not contain any point in
\mathcal{N}^{\circ}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)
.

If
b({\bf x})\notin\Lambda_{2}
, then
b({\bf x})\notin M(z)
for any
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)
, hence
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})(k)\cap\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{2}}(k)=\emptyset
.
∎

Corollary 9.3
.

Let
L\subset\mathbb{V}
. Assume
z\in\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L)(k)
and
z\in\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}(k)
where
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
. Then
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(\pi L).

Corollary 9.4
.

Assume
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}
and
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})>0
. Assume
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}
where
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
, then either
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(\frac{1}{\pi}{\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}
or
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(\frac{1}{\pi}{\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a unique superspecial point.

Lemma 9.5
.

For
L\subset\mathbb{V}
a lattice of arbitrary rank,
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L)_{\mathrm{red}}
is connected.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L)_{\mathrm{red}}
has two different connected components
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
. Since
\mathrm{SU}(\mathbb{V})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{L}
, we can find a
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}
such that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,1}
(i.e.
\{{\bf x}\}^{\bot}
is split) and
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}\cap U_{i}\neq\emptyset
for
i=1,2
. Hence the reduced locus of

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L\oplus\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}\})\cong\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,1}(L^{\prime})

is not connected where
L^{\prime}
is the orthogonal projection of
L
onto
\{{\bf x}\}^{\bot}
. This contradicts Corollaries 3.13, 3.15 and Lemma 3.16 of
[
HSY23
]
.
∎

Recall that for a lattice
L\subset\mathbb{V}
(respectively,
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}
), we have defined
\mathcal{V}(L)
and
\mathcal{L}(L)
(respectively,
\mathcal{V}({\bf x})
and
\mathcal{L}({\bf x})
) in Section
2.3
.

Proposition 9.6
.

Assume that
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}
such that
h({\bf x},{\bf x})\neq 0
. Then we have

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}({\bf x})}\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda},

where
\mathcal{V}({\bf x})
is given as follows.

(1)

When
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})=0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}\}^{\perp}
is non-split, there is a unique vertex lattice
\Lambda_{\bf x}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
containing
b({\bf x})
. In this case
\mathcal{V}({\bf x})=\{\Lambda_{\bf x}\}
.

(2)

When
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})=d
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}\}^{\perp}
is non-split, we have

\mathcal{V}({\bf x})=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\Lambda_{{\bf x}/\pi^{d}})\leq d\}

where
\Lambda_{{\bf x}/\pi^{d}}
is as in (1).

(3)

When
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})=0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}\}^{\perp}
is split,
\mathcal{L}({\bf x})
is the tree
\mathcal{L}_{2,1}
.

(4)

When

\mathrm{v}({\bf x})=d
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}\}^{\perp}
is split, we have

\mathcal{V}({\bf x})=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}({\bf x}/\pi^{d}))\leq d\}

where
\mathcal{L}({\bf x}/\pi^{d})
is as in (3).

(5)

When
h({\bf x},{\bf x})\notin\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}
,
\mathcal{V}({\bf x})
is empty.

Proof.

Proof of (1): This is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.6
and the fact that
\mathcal{N}_{2,-1}^{\mathrm{Pap}}
has only one reduced point, see
[
Shi22
, Section 2]
or
[
RSZ18
, Section 8]
. Alternatively since
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{b({\bf x})\}^{\perp}
is non-split of dimension
2
, it contains a unique self-dual lattice
\Lambda^{\prime}
, then
\Lambda_{\bf x}\coloneqq\mathrm{Span}\{b({\bf x})\}\oplus\Lambda^{\prime}
is the unique type
0
lattice containing
b({\bf x})
.

Proof of (3): Applying Proposition
2.6
,we see that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})\cong\mathcal{N}_{2,1}^{\mathrm{Pap}}
is the Drinfeld
p
-adic half space, see
[
KR14a
]
and
[
HSY23
]
. The required properties of
\mathcal{L}({\bf x})
and
\mathcal{V}({\bf x})
follow.

Proof of (2): We prove this by induction. The case
d=0
is just (1). Now we assume
d>0
and that the statement holds for
d-1
, i.e.

\mathcal{V}({\bf x}/\pi)=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\Lambda_{{\bf x}/\pi^{d}})\leq d-1\}.

Then applying Corollary
9.3
to the lattice
L=\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}/\pi\}
we have

\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2},\ d(\Lambda,\Lambda_{{\bf x}/\pi^{d}})\leq d}\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x})_{\mathrm{red}}.

Corollary
9.4
and the induction hypothesis imply that every
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}({\bf x})
satisfies
d(\Lambda,\Lambda_{{\bf x}/\pi^{d}})\leq d
. By Lemma
9.5
there is no isolated
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}({\bf x})
, i.e. every
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}({\bf x})
is contained in some
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}({\bf x})
if
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})>0
. This finishes the proof of (2).

Similarly we can prove (4) by an induction on
d
, the case
d=0
is just (3).

(5) follows directly from Lemma
9.2
.
∎

9.3.
Rank
2
case

Proposition 9.7
.

Assume that
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\}\subset\mathbb{V}
is integral of rank
2
. Then

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L^{\flat})_{\mathrm{red}}=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}(L^{\flat})}\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}

is a finite union, where
\mathcal{V}(L^{\flat})
is the set of vertices of the tree
\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})
described as follows.

(1)

Assume
L^{\flat}\approx\mathcal{H}_{2a+1}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then

\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})
is a ball centered at a vertex lattice of type
2
with radius
\frac{2a+1}{2}
.

(2)

Assume
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1},\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{2}\}
where
\mathrm{v}({\bf x}_{1})=0
,
\mathrm{v}({\bf x}_{2})\geq 0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}_{1}\}^{\bot}
is non-split. Then
\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})
is a ball centered at a vertex lattice of type
0
with radius
a
.

(3)

Assume
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1},\pi^{a+r}{\bf x}_{2}\}
where
{\bf x}_{1}\bot{\bf x}_{2}
,
\mathrm{v}({\bf x}_{1})=\mathrm{v}({\bf x}_{2})=0
,
r\geq 0
and
\mathrm{Span}_{F}\{{\bf x}_{1}\}^{\bot}
is split. Then

\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat}))\leq a\},

where

\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat})=\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1})\mid d(\Lambda,\Lambda_{0})\leq r\},

\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1})
is described in (3) of Proposition
9.6
and
\Lambda_{0}
is the unique type
0
vertex lattice containing
\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\}
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
9.2
, for a
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L^{\flat})_{\mathrm{red}}
if and only if
\Lambda^{\sharp}
contains
b({\bf x}_{1}),b({\bf x}_{2})
.

We first prove (1) when
a=0
. Suppose
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}(L^{\flat})
. Extend
\{b({\bf x}_{1}),b({\bf x}_{2})\}
to a basis
\{b({\bf x}_{1}),b({\bf x}_{2}),b_{3}\}
of
\mathbb{V}
with Gram matrix
\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\{-\epsilon\}
. Choose a basis
\{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}
of
\Lambda^{\sharp}
with the same Gram matrix
\mathcal{H}_{1}\oplus\{-\epsilon\}
. Then
b({\bf x}_{i})\in\Lambda^{\sharp}
(
i=1,2
) by Lemma
9.2
and

b({\bf x}_{i})=a_{i1}v_{1}+a_{i2}v_{2}+a_{i3}v_{3}

where
a_{ij}\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
(
j=1,2,3
). The fact that
(b({\bf x}_{i}),b({\bf x}_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}=T
implies
a_{i3}\in\pi\mathcal{O}_{F}
for
i=1,2
and
(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
is in
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{F})
. This guarantees that
L^{\flat}
is a direct summand of
\Lambda^{\sharp}
by Gram-Schmidt process.
Hence
\Lambda^{\sharp}
is, in fact, the lattice
\mathrm{Span}_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}\{b({\bf x}_{1}),b({\bf x}_{2}),b_{3}\}
. The fact that all
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L^{\flat})
are in
\Lambda
follows from Lemma
9.5
.

When
a=0
, (2) follows from the fact that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x}_{1})=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,-1}
(by Proposition
2.6
) and
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}(L^{\flat})_{\mathrm{red}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x}_{1})_{\mathrm{red}}
is a unique superspecial point.
Similarly when
a=0
, (3) follows from the fact that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Pap}}({\bf x}_{1})=\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,1}
and
[
HSY23
, Corollary 3.13]
.

Now we prove (1), (2) and (3) for general
a
. First of all,
\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})=\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{2})
by definition. By Corollary
9.3
we have

\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat}))\leq a\}\subset\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{2}).

Notice that for a sub-tree
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
of a tree
\mathcal{L}
and a vertex
x\in\mathcal{L}\setminus\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
, there is a unique geodesic segment joining
x
with
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
. Given
\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})=\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{2})
, let
\gamma
be the unique geodesic segment joining
\Lambda
with
\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat})
. Assume that
\gamma
intersects
\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat})
at
\Lambda(L^{\flat})
. Since
\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat})=\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{2})
,
\gamma
necessarily intersects both
\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{2})
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\gamma
intersects
\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1})
at
\Lambda({\bf x}_{1})
first. Hence the intersection of
\gamma
with
\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{2})
is
\Lambda(L^{\flat})
and

d(\Lambda,\Lambda({\bf x}_{1}))=d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1}))\leq d(\Lambda,\Lambda(L^{\flat}))=d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{2})).

Now by Proposition
9.6
, we have

d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{1}))\leq a,\ d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}({\bf x}_{2}))\leq a.

Hence
d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat}))\leq a
. This shows that

\{\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}\mid d(\Lambda,\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat}))\leq a\}=\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{L}(\pi^{a}{\bf x}_{2}).

The general case of (1), (2) and (3) follows from the above equation and the case
a=0
.

Notice that (1), (2) and (3) have covered all possibilities of
L^{\flat}
due to the classification of Hermitian lattices. Notice that in every case
\mathcal{V}(L^{\flat})
is finite. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Definition 9.8
.

Assume that
L^{\flat}
is an integral lattice of rank
2
in
\mathbb{V}
. Define
\mathcal{S}(L^{\flat})
, the skeleton of
\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})
, as follows.
If the fundamental invariant of
L^{\flat}
is
(2a,b)
(
b\geq 2a
), define
\mathcal{S}(L^{\flat})\coloneqq\mathcal{L}(\pi^{-a}L^{\flat})
. If the fundamental invariant of
L^{\flat}
is
(2a+1,2a+1)
, define
\mathcal{S}(L^{\flat})\coloneqq\emptyset
.

Remark 9.9
.

The skeleton
\mathcal{S}(L^{\flat})
is isomorphic to a ball in the Bruhat-Tits tree of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}_{2,\pm 1}
.

Corollary 9.10
.

For each
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}(L^{\flat})
not on the skeleton
\mathcal{S}(L^{\flat})
, one can find
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L^{\flat})
such that
\Lambda_{2}
has the largest distance to the boundary
\mathcal{B}(L^{\flat})
of
\mathcal{L}(L^{\flat})
among all type
2
lattices in
\mathcal{V}^{2}(L^{\flat})
containing
\Lambda_{0}
.

Proof.

Assume the fundamental invariant of
L^{\flat}
is
(2a,b)
or
(2a+1,2a+1)
.
Define
M^{\flat}\coloneqq\pi^{-a}L^{\flat}
. Let
b
be the unique integer such that
\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{L}(\pi^{b}M^{\flat})\setminus\mathcal{L}(\pi^{b-1}M^{\flat})
. Choose any
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{B}(\pi^{b}M^{\flat})
such that
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
. Then by Proposition
9.7
,
\Lambda_{0}
satisfies the assumption of the corollary.
∎

9.4.
The Krämer model

For
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
, let
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
be the strict transform of
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
under the blow-up
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}
. Since the strict transform of a regular curve along any of its closed point is an isomorphism, we know
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Lemma 9.11
.

For
\Lambda\neq\Lambda^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
do not intersect.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
do not intersect in
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Pap}}
, then obviously
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
do not intersect. Without loss of generality we can assume
\Lambda=\mathrm{Span}\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}=\mathrm{Span}\{\pi^{-1}e_{1},\pi e_{2},e_{3}\}
where the Gram matrix of
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
is
\mathrm{Diag}(\mathcal{H},\epsilon)
.
Take
{\bf x}_{0}=e_{3}
. Then by Proposition
9.7
, both
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
are in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,1}
. Now by
[
HSY23
, Lemma 5.3]
,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
do not intersect.
∎

Lemma 9.12
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
and
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}
. When
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
intersects properly with
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}
and

(9.4)

\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}})=1.

When
\Lambda_{0}
is not contained in
\Lambda
,
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
does not intersect with
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.

Proof.

First assume
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
. Since
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
is a strict transformation of a curve, it intersects the exceptional divisor properly.
Let
{\bf x}_{0}
be as in the proof of Lemma
9.11
. Then
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
is in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,1}
.

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}})

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}})

\displaystyle=\chi(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0}),\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\prime}}).

Here
\mathrm{Exc}^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}
is the exceptional divisor on
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})
corresponding to the rank
2
self-dual lattice

\Lambda^{\prime}=\{v\in\Lambda_{0}\mid v\bot{\bf x}_{0}\}.

By
[
HSY23
, Lemma 5.2(a)]
, we know
\chi(\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0}),\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{0})}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}^{\prime}})=1
. When
\Lambda_{0}
is not contained in
\Lambda
, the superspecial point
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}(k)
is not contained in
\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda}
, hence
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
does not intersect with
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}
.
∎

10.
Intersection of vertical components and special divisors

In this section we study the intersection of
\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}
and special divisors. The main result is Theorem
10.2
. To proceed we first study the decomposition of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})
when
\mathrm{v}(L^{\flat})=0
. Since
n
is odd, we can without loss of generality assume that
\chi(\mathbb{V})=\chi(C)=1
. In the rest of the paper, we identify
\mathbb{V}
with
C
by the isomorphism
b
defined in (
2.3
).

10.1.
Decomposition of
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})

Let
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\}
where
{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\in\mathbb{V}
are linearly independent and the Hermitian form restricted to
L
is non-degenerate.

Lemma 10.1
.

We have that
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=[\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{1})}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{2})}]\in K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
is in fact in
\mathrm{F}^{2}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
. Moreover we have the decomposition in
\mathrm{Gr}^{2}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})

(10.1)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{h}+{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}.

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{h}
is described in Theorem
4.2
and
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L)_{v}\in\mathrm{Gr}^{2}K_{0}^{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.10
,
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})
is Noetherian and has a decomposition

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{h}\cup\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}.

Expressing
\mathcal{Z}({\bf x}_{i})
(
i=1,2
) as in (
3.1
) and applying
Propositions
3.2
,
3.3
and Lemma
3.4
,
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})
equals

[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{2})}]+\sum_{\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L^{\flat})}(2m_{\Lambda_{0}}({\bf x}_{1})m_{\Lambda_{0}}({\bf x}_{2})+m_{\Lambda_{0}}({\bf x}_{1})+m_{\Lambda_{0}}({\bf x}_{2}))H_{\Lambda_{0}}.

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{h}
is contained in
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})\cap\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{2})
and has dimension
1
by Theorem
4.2
.
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})\cap\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{2})_{v}
also has dimension
1
as it is supported on the reduced locus of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}
by Lemma
2.11
and does not contain any exceptional divisor
\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}
. Hence

(10.2)

[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{2})}]=[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})\cap\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{2})}]\in\mathrm{F}^{2}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}),

see for example
[
Zha21
, Lemma B.2]
.
Hence, we know that
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})\in\mathrm{F}^{2}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
. The desired decomposition then follows from Theorem
4.2
.
∎

By Lemma
10.1
and (
2.9
) we know that
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}\in K^{\prime}_{0}(Y)
where we can take
Y
to be the reduced locus of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}
. By the Bruhat-Tits stratification of
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}
and the fact that
\mathrm{Gr}^{1}K_{0}^{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})\cong\mathrm{CH}^{1}(\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}})
is generated by
H_{\Lambda_{0}}
, we have the following decomposition in
\mathrm{Gr}^{2}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}})
:

(10.3)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}=\sum_{\Lambda_{2}\in\mathcal{V}^{2}(L^{\flat})}m(\Lambda_{2},L^{\flat})[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda_{2}}}]+\sum_{\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L^{\flat})}m(\Lambda_{0},L^{\flat})H_{\Lambda_{0}}.

We will determine the multiplicities
m(\Lambda_{2},L^{\flat})
and
m(\Lambda_{0},L^{\flat})
when
\mathrm{v}(L^{\flat})=0
in this section and deal with the general case in Section
11
.

Now assume
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\}
with Gram matrix
\mathrm{Diag}(u_{1},u_{2}(-\pi_{0})^{n})
with
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
. Applying Proposition
3.2
to
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{1})
, we find

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{2})}]+\sum_{\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}({\bf x}_{1})}[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{2})}].

By Proposition
2.6
, we know the intersection
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}_{2})
is proper and is isomorphic to
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\chi(u_{1})}({\bf x}_{2})
. Combining this with Corollary
3.5
we obtain

(10.4)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=i_{*}({}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\chi(u_{1})}({\bf x}_{2}))-\sum_{\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{V}^{0}(L^{\flat})}H_{\Lambda_{0}}.

where
i_{*}
is the map
\mathrm{Gr}^{1}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\chi(u_{1})})\rightarrow\mathrm{Gr}^{2}K_{0}(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{3,1})
induced by the closed immersion
i:\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,\chi(u_{1})}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{3,1}
.
Equation (
10.4
) reduces the problem of decomposing
{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})
in this case to
[
Shi22
, Theorem 4.5]
and
[
HSY23
, Theorem 4.1]
. We do not make the effort to write the complete result down, but instead look at two basic examples.

Let us begin by the case when
L^{\flat}
is unimodular.
By (
10.4
) and either
[
Shi22
, Theorem 4.5]
or
[
HSY23
, Theorem 4.1]
, we have

(10.5)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(L^{\flat})^{\circ}}]

in the notation of Theorem
4.2
.

Next consider
L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2}\}
with Gram matrix
\mathrm{Diag}(1,-u\pi_{0})
where
u\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
. Then
\mathrm{Span}\{{\bf x}_{1}\}^{\perp}
is split and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}({\bf x}_{1})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}}_{2,1}
. By Proposition
9.7
(3),
\mathcal{V}^{2}(L^{\flat})
consists of two adjacent lattices
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Moreover by
[
HSY23
, Theorem 4.1]
and (
10.4
), we have

(10.6)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})_{v}=[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}]+[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}]+H_{\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime}}.

10.2.
The intersection number

Assume
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
. For
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}\backslash\{0\}
, define

(10.7)

\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda}({\bf x})\coloneqq\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})}).

In this subsection we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 10.2
.

Let
\Lambda\in\mathcal{V}^{2}
and
{\bf x}\in\mathbb{V}\backslash\{0\}
. Then

\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda}({\bf x})=1_{\Lambda}({\bf x})

where
1_{\Lambda}
is the characteristic function of
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{V}
.

Corollary 10.3
.

Assume that
\Lambda_{0}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\Lambda\in\mathcal{L}_{2}
such that
\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda
. Then for any
y_{0}\in\Lambda_{0}\setminus\pi\Lambda_{0}
such that
y_{0}^{\bot}
is non-split, we have

\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y_{0})})=0.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
, we know

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(y_{0})=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(y_{0})+\mathrm{Exc}_{\Lambda_{0}}.

Now the corollary follows immediately from Theorem
10.2
and Lemma
9.12
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.2
:

We consider three different cases.
First if
x\notin\Lambda
or
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})<0
, then by Lemma
9.2
,
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})\cap\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}=\emptyset
hence
\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda}({\bf x})=0
. From now on we assume
x\in\Lambda
and
\mathrm{v}({\bf x})\geq 0
.
Write
{\bf x}={\bf x}_{0}\pi^{n}
with
{\bf x}_{0}\in\Lambda\backslash\pi\Lambda
and
n\geq 0
.

Case 1
: First we assume
{\bf x}_{0}\in\Lambda\backslash\Lambda^{\sharp}
. Choose a basis
\{e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},e^{\prime}_{3}\}
of
\Lambda
with Gram matrix
\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{1}
such that

{\bf x}_{0}=xe^{\prime}_{1}+ye^{\prime}_{2}+ze^{\prime}_{3}.

Then one of
x
and
y
is in
\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
as
\Lambda^{\sharp}=\mathrm{Span}\{\pi e^{\prime}_{1},\pi e^{\prime}_{2},e_{3}\}
.
Apparently the equation

2u-v\bar{v}=h({\bf x}_{0},{\bf x}_{0})

has a solution
(u,v)\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{2}
with
u\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
. Now according to Lemma
A.2
, we can find a matrix
g\in\mathrm{U}(\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{1})(\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}})
such that

g\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\\
z\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\pi u\\
1\\
v\end{array}\right).

Now replace the basis
\{e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},e^{\prime}_{3}\}
by
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}=\{e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2},e^{\prime}_{3}\}g^{-1}
, we have

{\bf x}_{0}=\pi ue_{1}+e_{2}+ve_{3}

where
u\in\mathcal{O}_{F_{0}}^{\times}
,
v\in\mathcal{O}_{F}
.

Define

(10.8)

f_{1}=\frac{1}{\pi}u^{-1}e_{2},\ f_{2}=\pi ue_{1},\ f_{3}=e_{3}.

Then
\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
has also Gram matrix
\mathcal{H}_{3,1}^{1}
and
\Lambda^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathrm{Span}\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
is a type
2
lattice adjacent to
\Lambda
with
\Lambda_{c}=\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime}=\mathrm{Span}\{\pi e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
is a type
0
lattice. Now in terms of the basis
\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
we have

{\bf x}=\pi^{n}(\pi uf_{1}+f_{2}+vf_{3}).

Define
\theta\in\mathrm{U}(\mathbb{V})
by taking the basis
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\}
to
\{f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\}
. Then

\theta({\bf x})={\bf x},\quad\theta(\Lambda)=\Lambda^{\prime}.

In particular
\theta(\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}))=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})
and

(10.9)

\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}({\bf x})=\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda}({\bf x}).

Now let

\mathbf{y}_{0}=e_{3},\quad\mathbf{y}_{1}=\pi(-\pi ue_{1}+e_{2}),

L^{\flat}=\mathrm{Span}\{\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1}\}
, and
L=\mathrm{Span}\{\mathbf{y}_{0},\mathbf{y}_{1},{\bf x}\}
. Then by (
10.6
) and Theorem
4.2
we have

(10.10)

{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(L^{\flat})=[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda}}]+[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{N}}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}}]+H_{\Lambda_{c}}+[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})}].

where
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})
is the quasi-canonical lifting cycle of the lattice

M^{\flat}\coloneqq\mathrm{Span}\{e_{3},-\pi ue_{1}+e_{2}\}.

Combining with (
10.9
), we have

(10.11)

\mathrm{Int}(L)=2\cdot\mathrm{Int}_{\Lambda}({\bf x})+\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})\cdot H_{\Lambda_{c}})+\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})\cdot[\mathcal{O}_{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(M^{\flat})}]).

Let
{\bf x}^{\prime}=\pi^{n}(\pi ue_{1}+e_{2})={\bf x}-\pi^{n}ve_{3}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Int}(L)

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(\mathbf{y}_{0})\cdot{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x})\cdot{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(\mathbf{y}_{1}))

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{Kra}},{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(\mathbf{y}_{0})\cdot{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}({\bf x}^{\prime})\cdot{}^{\mathbb{L}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{Kra}}(\mathbf{y}_{1}))
